Good Wild Harmonic Bundles and Good Filtered Higgs Bundles This paper is a contribution to the Special Issue on Primitive Forms and Related Topics in honor of Kyoji Saito for his 77th birthday. The full collection is available at https://www.emis.de/journals/SIGMA/Saito.html
Takuro MOCHIZUKI

Abstract
We prove the Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence between good wild harmonic bundles and polystable good filtered λ \lambda -flat bundles satisfying a vanishing condition. We also study the correspondence for good wild harmonic bundles with the homogeneity with respect to a group action, which is expected to provide another way to construct Frobenius manifolds.



keywords
wild harmonic bundles; Higgs bundles;
\lambda
-flat bundles; Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence

AMS
53C07; 58E15; 14D21; 81T13

1
Introduction

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety
with a simple normal crossing hypersurface
H
.
Let
L
be an ample line bundle on
X
. We shall prove the following theorem,
that is the Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence
for good wild harmonic bundles
and good filtered
\lambda
-flat bundles.

Theorem 1.1

(Corollary

2.24
)
.

The following objects are equivalent:

•

Good wild harmonic bundles on
(X,H)
.

•

\mu_{L}
-Polystable filtered
\lambda
-flat bundles

\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)

on
(X,H)

satisfying

\displaystyle\int c_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V})c_{1}(L)^{\dim X-1}=0,\qquad\int\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V})c_{1}(L)^{\dim X-2}=0.

We shall recall the precise definitions
of the objects in Section
2
.

In
[
51
]
,
we have already proved that
good wild harmonic bundles on
(X,H)

induce
\mu_{L}
-polystable good filtered
\lambda
-flat bundles
satisfying the vanishing condition.
Note that
0
-flat bundles are equivalent to Higgs bundles,
and
1
-flat bundles are flat bundles in the ordinary sense.
Moreover,
we studied an analogue of Theorem
1.1

in the case
\lambda=1
,
i.e., the correspondence between
good wild harmonic bundles
and
\mu_{L}
-polystable good filtered flat bundles
satisfying a similar vanishing condition
[
51
, Theorem 16.1.1]
.
It was applied to the study of the correspondence
between semisimple algebraic holonomic
D
-modules
and pure twistor
D
-modules.

In this paper, as a complement,
we shall explain the proof for all
\lambda
.
There is no new essential difficulty
to prove Theorem
1.1

after our studies
[
46
,
47
,
48
,
49
,
51
]

on the basis of
[
62
,
63
]
.
Moreover, in some parts of the proof,
the arguments can be simplified in the Higgs case.
However,
because the Higgs case is also particularly important,
it would be useful to explain a rather detailed proof. We shall also explain the correspondences in homogeneous cases
which would be useful in a generalized Hodge theory.

1.1
Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondences

1.1.1
Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence for vector bundles

We briefly recall a part of the history of this type of correspondences.
(See also
[
25
,
35
,
41
]
.)
For a holomorphic vector bundle
E
on a compact Riemann surface
C
,
we set
\mu(E):=\deg(E)/\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits(E)
,
which is called the slope of
E
.
A holomorphic bundle
E
is called stable (resp. semistable)
if
\mu(E^{\prime})<\mu(E)
(resp.
\mu(E^{\prime})\leq\mu(E)
)
holds for any holomorphic subbundle

E^{\prime}\subset E
such that
0<\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits(E^{\prime})<\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits(E)
.
It is called polystable if
it is a direct sum of stable subbundles with the same slope.
This stability, semistability and polystability conditions
were introduced by Mumford
[
56
]

for the construction of the moduli spaces
of vector bundles with reasonable properties.
Narasimhan and Seshadri
[
58
]

established the equivalence between unitary flat bundles
and polystable bundles of degree
0
on compact Riemann surfaces.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a compact connected Kähler manifold.
For any torsion-free
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{F}
,
the slope of
\mathcal{F}
with respect to
\omega

is defined as

\displaystyle\mu_{\omega}(\mathcal{F}):=\frac{\int_{X}c_{1}(\mathcal{F})\omega^{\dim X-1}}{\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits\mathcal{F}}.

If the cohomology class of
\omega
is
the first Chern class of an ample line bundle
L
,
then
\mu_{\omega}(\mathcal{F})
is also denoted by
\mu_{L}(\mathcal{F})
.
Then, a torsion-free
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{F}
is called

\mu_{\omega}
-stable
if

\mu_{\omega}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})<\mu_{\omega}(\mathcal{F})
holds
for any saturated coherent subsheaf
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{F}

such that
0<\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})<\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits(\mathcal{F})
.
This condition was first studied by
Takemoto
[
71
,
72
]
.
It is also called

\mu_{\omega}
-stability, or slope stability.
Slope semistability and slope polystability
are naturally defined.

Bogomolov
[
4
]

introduced the
T
-stability condition for torsion-free sheaves
on connected projective surfaces,
and he proved the inequality of the Chern classes

c_{2}(E)-(r-1)c_{1}(E)^{2}/2r\geq 0

for any
T
-semistable bundle
E
of rank
r
. We do not recall the precise definition of

T
-stability condition here,
but we note that if a holomorphic vector bundle
on a complex projective manifold is slope semistable,
then it is
T
-semistable.
(See
[
4
, Section 7]
for more details.)
Gieseker
[
19
]
gave a different proof
of the inequality for slope semistable bundles.
The inequality is called Bogomolov–Gieseker inequality
or Bogomolov inequality.

Inspired by these works,
Kobayashi
[
32
]
introduced
the concept of Hermitian–Einstein condition
for metrics of holomorphic vector bundles.
Let
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E}\big)
be a holomorphic vector bundle on
a Kähler manifold
(X,\omega)
.
Let
h
be a Hermitian metric of
E
.
Let
R(h)
denote the curvature of the Chern connection

\nabla_{h}=\overline{\partial}_{E}+\partial_{E,h}
,
associated with
h
and
\overline{\partial}_{E}
.
Then,
h
is called Hermitian–Einstein
if
\Lambda R(h)^{\bot}=0
,
where
R(h)^{\bot}
denotes the trace-free part of
R(h)
. In particular,
he proved in
[
32
]
that
if a holomorphic vector bundle on a compact Kähler manifold
has a Hermitian–Einstein metric,
then it is
T
-semistable.
Kobayashi
[
33
,
34
]

and Lübke
[
40
]

proved that
a holomorphic vector bundle
on a compact connected Kähler manifold
satisfies the slope polystability condition
if it has a Hermitian–Einstein metric.
Moreover,
Lübke
[
39
]
established
the so called Kobayashi–Lübke inequality
for the first and the second Chern forms
associated with Hermitian–Einstein metrics,
which is reduced to the inequality

\mathop{\rm Tr}\nolimits\bigl(\big(R(h)^{\bot}\big)^{2}\bigr)\omega^{\dim X-2}\geq 0

in the form level. In particular, it implies
the Bogomolov–Gieseker inequality
for holomorphic vector bundles
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E}\big)

with a Hermitian–Einstein metric
h
on compact Kähler manifolds

(X,\omega)
.
Moreover,
if
c_{1}(E)=0
and
\int_{X}\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{2}(E)\omega^{\dim X-2}=0

are satisfied
for such
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},h\big)
,
and if we impose
\det(h)
is flat,
then the Kobayashi–Lübke inequality implies
that
R(h)=0
,
i.e.,
\nabla_{h}
is flat.

Independently, in
[
36
]
,
Hitchin proposed a problem
to ask an equivalence of the stability condition
and the existence of a metric
h
such that
\Lambda R(h)=0
,
under the vanishing of the first Chern class of the bundle.
(See
[
25
]
for more precise explanation.)
It clearly contains the most important essence.
He also suggested possible applications
of the vanishings.
His problem stimulated Donaldson
whose work on this topic
brought several breakthroughs to
whole geometry.

In
[
14
]
,
Donaldson introduced the method of global analysis
to reprove the theorem of Narasimhan–Seshadri.
In
[
15
]
,
by using the method of the heat flow associated with
the Hermitian–Einstein condition,
he established the equivalence of
the slope polystability condition
and the existence of a Hermitian–Einstein metric
for holomorphic vector bundles on any complex projective surface.
The important concept of Donaldson functional was
also introduced in
[
15
]
.

Eventually, Donaldson
[
16
]
and
Uhlenbeck–Yau
[
73
]

established the equivalence
on any dimensional complex projective manifolds.
Note that Uhlenbeck–Yau proved it for any compact Kähler manifolds,
more generally.
The correspondence is called
with various names;
Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence,
Hitchin–Kobayashi correspondence,
Donaldson–Hitchin–Uhlenbeck–Yau correspondence, etc. In this paper, we call it the Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence.

As a consequence of the Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence
and the Kobayashi–Lübke inequality,
we also obtain an equivalence between
unitary flat bundles
and slope polystable holomorphic vector bundles
E

satisfying
\mu_{\omega}(E)=0
and
\int_{X}\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{2}(E)\omega^{\dim X-2}=0
.
Note that Mehta and Ramanathan
[
43
,
44
]

deduced the equivalence on complex projective manifolds
directly from the equivalence in the surface case due to Donaldson

[
15
]
.

1.1.2
Higgs bundles and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
-flat bundles

Such correspondences have been also studied for
vector bundles equipped with some additional structure,
which are also called Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondences in this paper.
One of the most rich and influential is the case of Higgs bundles,
pioneered by Hitchin and Simpson.

Let
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E}\big)
be a holomorphic vector bundle
on a compact Riemann surface
C
.
A Higgs field of
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E}\big)
is a holomorphic section

\theta
of
\mathop{\rm End}\nolimits(E)\otimes\Omega^{1}_{C}
.
Let
h
be a Hermitian metric of
E
. We obtain the Chern connection
\overline{\partial}_{E}+\partial_{E,h}

and its curvature
R(h)
.
Let
\theta^{\dagger}_{h}

denote the adjoint of
\theta
.
In
[
24
]
,
Hitchin introduced the following equation,
called the Hitchin equation,

\displaystyle R(h)+\big[\theta,\theta_{h}^{\dagger}\big]=0.

(1.1)

Such
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h\big)
is called a harmonic bundle. In particular,
he studied the case
\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits E=2
.
Among many deep results in
[
24
]
,
he proved that
a Higgs bundle
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta\big)
has
a Hermitian metric
h
satisfying (
1.1
)
if and only if
it is polystable of degree
0
.
Here, a Higgs bundle
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta\big)
is called stable
(resp. semistable)
if
\mu(E^{\prime})<\mu(E)

(resp.
\mu(E^{\prime})\leq\mu^{\prime}(E)
) holds for any
holomorphic subbundle
E^{\prime}\subset E

such that
\theta(E^{\prime})\subset E^{\prime}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{C}

and that
0<\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits(E^{\prime})<\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits(E)
,
and a Higgs bundle is called polystable
if it is a direct sum of stable Higgs subbundles
with the same slope. By this equivalence and another equivalence due to Donaldson
[
17
]

between irreducible flat bundles
and twisted harmonic maps,
Hitchin obtained that the moduli space
of polystable Higgs bundles of degree
0

and the moduli space of semisimple flat bundles
are isomorphic.
His work revealed that the moduli spaces
of Higgs bundles and flat bundles
have extremely rich structures.

The higher dimensional case was studied by Simpson
[
62
]
.
Note that Simpson started his study independently
motivated by a new way to construct variations of Hodge structure,
which we shall mention later in Section
1.2.1
.
For a holomorphic vector bundle
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E}\big)

on a complex manifold
X
with arbitrary dimension,
a Higgs field
\theta
is defined to be
a holomorphic section of
\mathop{\rm End}\nolimits(E)\otimes\Omega^{1}_{X}

satisfying the additional condition
\theta\wedge\theta=0
.
Suppose that
X
has a Kähler form.
Let
h
be a Hermitian metric of
E
.
Let
F(h)
denote the curvature of the connection

\nabla_{h}+\theta+\theta_{h}^{\dagger}
.
A Hermitian metric
h
of a Higgs bundle
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta\big)

is called Hermitian–Einstein
if
\Lambda F(h)^{\bot}=0
.
When
X
is compact,
the slope stability, semistability and polystability
conditions for Higgs bundles
are naturally defined
in terms of the slopes of Higgs subsheaves.
Simpson established that
a Higgs bundle
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta\big)
on a compact Kähler manifold

(X,\omega)
has a Hermitian–Einstein metric
if and only if it is slope polystable.
Moreover, he generalized the Kobayashi–Lübke inequality
for the Chern forms to the context of Higgs bundles,
which is reduced to the inequality

\mathop{\rm Tr}\nolimits\bigl(\big(F(h)^{\bot}\big)^{2}\bigr)\omega^{\dim X-2}\geq 0

in the form level
for any Hermitian–Einstein metric
h
of
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta\big)
.
Here, the condition
\theta\wedge\theta=0
is essential. In particular, it implies that
if
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta\big)
on a compact Kähler manifold
(X,\omega)

satisfies
\mu_{\omega}(E)=0
and
\int_{X}\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{2}(E)\omega^{\dim X-2}=0
,
then a Hermitian–Einstein metric
h
of
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta\big)

is
a pluri-harmonic metric
,
i.e., the connection
\nabla_{h}+\theta+\theta_{h}^{\dagger}
is flat.
It is equivalent to the following:

\displaystyle\partial_{E,h}\theta=0,\qquad\overline{\partial}\theta_{h}^{\dagger}=0,\qquad R(h)+\big[\theta,\theta_{h}^{\dagger}\big]=0.

A Higgs bundle
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta\big)

with a pluri-harmonic metric is called a harmonic bundle.
This equivalence
and another important equivalence due to Corlette
[
11
]

induce an equivalence between
semisimple flat bundles and polystable Higgs bundles

\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta\big)

satisfying
\mu_{\omega}(E)=0
and
\int_{X}\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{2}(E)\omega^{\dim X-2}=0

on any connected compact Kähler manifold.

After the work of Corlette, Donaldson, Hitchin and Simpson,
it turned out that the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(X)

of flat bundles on a complex projective manifold
X

has a hyper-Kähler metric. In particular, it induces the twistor space of the moduli space

\mathop{\rm TW}\nolimits(\mathcal{M}(X))
,
which is a complex analytic space with a fibration

\mathop{\rm TW}\nolimits(\mathcal{M}(X))\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
such that the fiber over
1
is the moduli space of
flat bundles,
and that the fiber over
0
is the moduli space of
Higgs bundles with vanishing rational Chern classes.
The notion of
\lambda
-connections
was introduced and developed by
Deligne and Simpson
[
65
,
66
]

for a more complex analytic construction
of the twistor space
\mathop{\rm TW}\nolimits(\mathcal{M}(X))
.
They obtain the family
of the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}^{\lambda}(X)

of
\lambda
-flat bundles on
X
,
and the family of the moduli spaces

\mathcal{M}^{\mu}\big(X^{\dagger}\big)

of
\mu
-flat bundles on
the conjugate
X^{\dagger}
of
X
.
They proved that the twistor space
\mathop{\rm TW}\nolimits(\mathcal{M}(X))

can be obtained as the gluing of
the two families

\coprod_{\lambda}\mathcal{M}^{\lambda}(X)

and
\coprod_{\mu}\mathcal{M}^{\mu}\big(X^{\dagger}\big)

by the natural identification of

\mathcal{M}^{\lambda}(X)=\mathcal{M}^{\mu}\big(X^{\dagger}\big)

for
\lambda\mu=1
.

These correspondences are not only really interesting,
but also provide a starting point of the further investigations.
Simpson pursued the comparison of flat bundles,
Higgs bundles
and more generally
\lambda
-flat bundles
in deeper levels
[
64
,
66
]
,
and developed the non-abelian Hodge theory
[
65
]
. In particular, he explained that
the Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondences
for
\lambda
-flat bundles can be
studied in a unified way
[
64
]
.
For more recent study on the moduli spaces of
\lambda
-connections,
see
[
10
,
26
,
27
,
67
]
,
for example.

1.1.3
Filtered case

It is interesting to generalize such correspondences
for objects on complex quasi-projective manif̃olds. We need to impose a kind of boundary condition,
that is parabolic structure.

Mehta and Seshadri
[
45
]

introduced the concept of parabolic structure of vector bundles
on compact Riemann surfaces.
Let
C
be a compact Riemann surface
with a finite subset
D\subset C
.
Let
E
be a holomorphic vector bundle on
C
.
A parabolic structure of
E
at
D

is a tuple of filtrations

F_{\bullet}(E_{|P})

(P\in D)

indexed by
]{-}1,0]

satisfying

F_{a}(E_{|P})=\bigcap_{b>a}F_{b}(E_{|P})
.
Set

\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{a}(E_{|P}):=F_{a}(E)/F_{<a}(E)
,
and

\displaystyle\deg(E,F):=\deg(E)-\sum_{P\in D}\sum_{-1<a\leq 0}a\dim\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{a}(E_{|P}).

We set
\mu(E,F):=\deg(E,F)/\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits(E)
.
For any subbundle
E^{\prime}\subset E
,
filtrations
F(E^{\prime}_{|P})
on
E^{\prime}_{|P}

are induced as

F_{a}(E^{\prime}_{|P}):=F_{a}(E_{|P})\cap E^{\prime}_{|P}
.
Then,
(E,F)
is called stable
if
\mu(E^{\prime},F)<\mu(E,F)

for any subbundle
E^{\prime}\subset E

with
0<\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits(E^{\prime})<\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits(E)
.
Semistability and polystability conditions
are also defined naturally.
Then, Mehta and Seshadri proved an equivalence of
irreducible unitary flat bundles on
C\setminus D

and stable parabolic vector bundles
(E,F)
with
\mu(E,F)=0

on
(C,D)
.

For some purposes,
it is more convenient to replace
parabolic bundles with filtered bundles
introduced by Simpson
[
62
,
63
]
.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{C}(\ast D)
denote the sheaf of
meromorphic functions on
C
which may have poles along
D
.
Let
\mathcal{V}
be a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{C}(\ast D)
-module.
A filtered bundle
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}
over
\mathcal{V}

is a tuple of lattices

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}

({\boldsymbol{a}}=(a_{P})_{P\in D}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D})

such that

(i)

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}(\ast D)=\mathcal{V}
,

(ii)
the restriction of
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}

to a neighbourhood of
P\in D
depends only on
a_{P}
,

(iii)

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}+{\boldsymbol{n}}}\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}\big(\sum n_{P}P\big)

for any
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and
{\boldsymbol{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}
,

(iv)
for any
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, there exists

{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{D}
such that

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}+{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}}\mathcal{V}
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{0}}
denote
(0,\ldots,0)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
.
Then,
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{0}}}\mathcal{V}
is equipped with
the parabolic structure
F

induced by the images
of
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}_{|P}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{0}}}\mathcal{V}_{|P}

(P\in D)
.
It is easy to observe that
filtered bundles are equivalent to parabolic bundles. We set

\mu(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}):=\mu(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{0}}}\mathcal{V},F)

for the filtered bundle
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}
.

Simpson
[
62
,
63
]
generalized the theorem of
Mehta-Seshadri to the correspondences of
tame harmonic bundles
and regular filtered
\lambda
-flat bundles
on compact Riemann surfaces.
A harmonic bundle
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h\big)
on
C\setminus D

is called tame on
(C,D)

if the closure of the spectral curve of
\theta

in
T^{\ast}C(\log D)
is proper over
C
.
A regular filtered
\lambda
-flat bundle consists of
a filtered bundle
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}

equipped with a flat
\lambda
-connection

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\colon\mathcal{V}\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{C}

such that

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\cdot\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{C}(\log D)

for any
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
.
Stability, semistable and polystable conditions
are naturally defined in terms of the slope.
Then, Simpson established the equivalence of
tame harmonic bundles on
(C,D)

and polystable regular filtered
\lambda
-flat bundles

\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)
satisfying

\mu(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V})=0
.
Note that filtered bundles express
the growth order of the norms of holomorphic sections
with respect to the metrics. We should mention that
the study of the asymptotic behaviour of
tame harmonic bundles is much harder than
that of the asymptotic behaviour of unitary flat bundles.
Hence,
it is already hard to prove that
tame harmonic bundles induce
regular filtered
\lambda
-flat bundles.

There are several directions to generalize.
One is a generalization in the context of
tame harmonic bundles on higher dimensional varieties.
Let
X
be a smooth connected projective variety
with a simple normal crossing hypersurface
H

and an ample line bundle
L
.
Then, there should be equivalences of
tame harmonic bundles on
(X,H)

and
\mu_{L}
-polystable regular filtered
\lambda
-flat bundles

(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V},\theta)

on
(X,H)
satisfying

\int_{X}c_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V})c_{1}(L)^{\dim X-1}=0

and
\int_{X}\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V})c_{1}(L)^{\dim X-2}=0

for each
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
.
In
[
2
]
, Biquard studied the case where
H
is smooth.
In
[
37
,
38
,
70
]
,
Li, Narasimhan, Steer and Wren studied
the correspondence for parabolic bundles
without flat
\lambda
-connections.
In
[
30
]
, Jost and Zuo studied
the correspondence between semisimple flat bundles
and tame harmonic bundles.
In
[
46
,
47
,
48
,
49
]
,
the author obtained the satisfactory equivalences
for tame harmonic bundles.
Note that Donagi and Pantev recently proposed
an attractive application of the Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence
for tame harmonic bundles to the study of geometric Langlands theory

[
13
]
.

In another natural direction of generalization,
we should consider more singular objects
than regular filtered Higgs or flat bundles.
A harmonic bundle
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h\big)

on
X\setminus H
is called
wild if the closure of the spectral variety of

\theta

in the projective completion of
T^{\ast}X
is complex analytic.
For the analysis,
we should impose that
the spectral variety of the harmonic bundle
satisfies some non-degeneracy condition along
H
.
(See Section
2.7.1
.)
This is not essential because the condition is always satisfied
once we replace
X
by its appropriate blow up.
The notion of regular filtered
\lambda
-flat bundle
is appropriately generalized to the notion of
good filtered
\lambda
-flat bundle.
The results of Simpson should be generalized to
equivalences of good wild harmonic bundles
and
\mu_{L}
-polystable good filtered
\lambda
-flat bundles

\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)

satisfying

\int_{X}c_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V})c_{1}(L)^{\dim X-1}=0

and
\int_{X}\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V})c_{2}(L)^{\dim X-2}=0
.
Sabbah
[
59
]
studied
the correspondence between
semisimple meromorphic flat bundles
and wild harmonic bundles
in the one dimensional case.
Biquard and Boalch
[
3
]

obtained generalization for wild harmonic bundles
in the one dimensional case.
Boalch informed the author that wild generalization
in the context of the Higgs case was not expected in those days.

As mentioned, the author studied
the wild harmonic bundles
on any dimensional varieties
in
[
51
]
. We obtained that good wild harmonic bundles
induce
\mu_{L}
-polystable good filtered
\lambda
-flat bundles
satisfying the vanishing conditions.
Moreover,
we proved that the construction induces an equivalence
of good wild harmonic bundles
and slope polystable good filtered flat bundles
satisfying the vanishing condition.
Such an equivalence for meromorphic flat bundles
is particularly interesting
because we may apply it to prove a conjecture of Kashiwara
[
31
]

on semisimple algebraic holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-modules.
See a survey paper

[
54
]
for more details on this application.

In
[
51
]
,
we did not give a proof of the equivalence
for wild harmonic bundles
in the case
\lambda\neq 1

because it is rather obvious that a similar argument can work
after
[
46
,
47
,
48
,
49
,
51
]

on the basis of
[
62
,
63
]
.
But, because the Higgs case is also important,
it would be better to have a reference
in which a rather detailed proof is explained.
It is one reason why the author writes this manuscript.
As another reason, in the next subsection,
we shall explain an application
to the correspondence for good wild harmonic bundles
with homogeneity,
which is expected to be useful in the generalized Hodge theory.

1.2
Homogeneity with respect to group actions

1.2.1
Variation of Hodge structure

As mentioned, Simpson
[
62
]
was motivated by
the construction of polarized variation of Hodge structure.
Let us recall the definition of polarized complex variation of
Hodge structure given in
[
62
]
,
instead of the original definition of
polarized variation of Hodge structure due to Griffiths.
A complex variation of Hodge structure of weight
w

is a graded
C^{\infty}
-vector bundle

V=\bigoplus_{p+q=w}V^{p,q}

equipped with a flat connection
\nabla

satisfying the Griffiths transversality condition,
i.e.,

\nabla^{0,1}(V^{p,q})\subset\Omega^{0,1}\otimes\bigl(V^{p+1,q-1}\oplus V^{p,q}\bigr)

and

\nabla^{1,0}(V^{p,q})\subset\Omega^{1,0}\otimes\bigl(V^{p-1,q+1}\oplus V^{p,q}\bigr)
,
where
\nabla^{p,q}
denote the
(p,q)
-part
of
\nabla
.
A polarization of a complex variation of Hodge structure
is a flat Hermitian pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle

satisfying the following conditions:

(i)
the decomposition
V=\bigoplus V^{p,q}
is orthogonal
with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
,

(ii)

\big(\sqrt{-1}\big)^{p-q}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle

is positive definite on
V^{p,q}
.

A polarization of pure Hodge structure
typically appears
when we consider the Gauss–Manin connection
associated with
a smooth projective morphism
f\colon\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Y}
.
Namely,
the family of vector spaces

H^{w}\big(f^{-1}(y)\big)

(y\in\mathcal{Y})

naturally induces a flat bundle on
\mathcal{Y}
.
With the Hodge decomposition,
it is a variation of Hodge structure of weight
w
.
A relatively ample line bundle
induces a polarization on the variation of Hodge structure.

Simpson discovered a completely different way to construct
a polarized variation of Hodge structure.
Let
(V=\bigoplus V^{p,q},\nabla)

be a complex variation of Hodge structure.
Note that
\nabla^{0,1}
induces
holomorphic structures

\overline{\partial}_{V^{p,q}}\colon V^{p,q}\longrightarrow V^{p,q}\otimes\Omega^{0,1}

of
V^{p,q}
. We set

\overline{\partial}_{V}:=\bigoplus\overline{\partial}_{V^{p,q}}
.
Then,

\big(V=\bigoplus V^{p,q},\overline{\partial}_{V}\big)
is
a graded holomorphic vector bundle. We also note that
\nabla^{1,0}
induces
linear maps
V^{p,q}\longrightarrow V^{p-1,q+1}\otimes\Omega^{1,0}
,
and hence

\theta\colon V\longrightarrow V\otimes\Omega^{1,0}
.
It is easy to check that

\theta
is a Higgs field of
\big(V,\overline{\partial}_{V}\big)
.
Such a graded holomorphic bundle

V=\bigoplus_{p+q=w}V^{p,q}

with a Higgs field
\theta
such that

\theta(V^{p,q})\subset V^{p-1,q+1}\otimes\Omega^{1,0}

is called a Hodge bundle of weight
w
. In general, we cannot construct
a complex variation of Hodge structure
from a Hodge bundle.
However, Simpson discovered that
if a Hodge bundle
(V=\bigoplus V^{p,q},\theta)

on a compact Kähler manifold
satisfies the stability condition and the vanishing condition,
then there exists a flat connection
\nabla

and a flat Hermitian pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle

such that

(i)

(V=\bigoplus V^{p,q},\nabla)
is a complex variation
of Hodge structure which induces the Hodge bundle,

(ii)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is a polarization
of
(V=\bigoplus V^{p,q},\nabla)
.
Indeed, according to the equivalence of Simpson
between Higgs bundles and harmonic bundles,
there exists a pluri-harmonic metric
h
of
(V,\theta)
.
It turns out that
the flat connection

\nabla_{h}+\theta+\theta^{\dagger}_{h}

satisfies the Griffiths transversality.
Moreover,
the decomposition
V=\bigoplus V^{p,q}
is orthogonal
with respect to
h
,
and flat Hermitian paring
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle

is constructed by the relation

\big(\sqrt{-1}\big)^{p-q}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{V^{p,q}}=h_{|V^{p,q}}
.

Note that a Hodge bundle
is regarded as a Higgs bundle
\big(V,\overline{\partial}_{V},\theta\big)

with an
S^{1}
-homogeneity,
i.e.,
\big(V,\overline{\partial}_{V}\big)
is equipped with an
S^{1}
-action
such that
t\circ\theta\circ t^{-1}=t\cdot\theta

for any
t\in S^{1}
.
It roughly means that
semistable Hodge bundles correspond to
the fixed points in the moduli space of semistable Higgs bundles
with respect to the natural
S^{1}
-action
induced by
t\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta\big)=\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},t\theta\big)
.

By the deformation

\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\alpha\theta\big)

(\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast})
,
any semistable Higgs bundles is deformed to
an
S^{1}
-fixed point in the moduli space,
i.e., a semistable Hodge bundle as
\alpha\to 0
.
Note that the Higgs field of the limit is not necessarily
0
.
Hence, by the equivalence between Higgs bundles and flat bundles,
it turns out that
any flat bundle is deformed to a flat bundle underlying
a polarized variation of Hodge structure.

In particular, Simpson
[
62
]
applied these ideas
to construct uniformizations
of some types of project̄ive manifolds.
He also applied it to prove that
some type of discrete groups cannot be
the fundamental group of any projective manifolds
in
[
64
]
.

1.2.2
TE-structure

We recall that a complex variation of Hodge structure
on
X
induces
a TE-structure in the sense of Hertling
[
21
]
,
i.e.,
a holomorphic vector bundle
\mathcal{V}

on
\mathcal{X}:={\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\times X

with a meromorphic flat connection

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nabla}\colon\ \mathcal{V}\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}\big(\mathcal{X}^{0}\big)\otimes\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\big(\log\mathcal{X}^{0}\big),

where
\mathcal{X}^{0}:=\{0\}\times X
.
Indeed,
for a complex variation of Hodge structure

(V=\bigoplus V^{p,q},\nabla)
,

F^{p}(V):=\bigoplus_{p_{1}\geq p}V^{p_{1},q_{1}}

are holomorphic subbundles
with respect to
\nabla^{0,1}
.
Thus, we obtain a decreasing filtration
of holomorphic subbundles

F^{p}(V)

(p\in{\mathbb{Z}})

satisfying the Griffiths transversality

\nabla^{1,0}F^{p}(V)\subset F^{p-1}(V)\otimes\Omega^{1,0}
.
Let
p\colon{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}_{\lambda}\times X\longrightarrow X

denote the projection. We obtain the induced flat bundle
(p^{\ast}V,p^{\ast}\nabla)
. By the Rees construction,

p^{\ast}V
extends to a locally free

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-module
\mathcal{V}
,
on which
\widetilde{\nabla}:=p^{\ast}\nabla
is a meromorphic flat connection
satisfying the condition

\widetilde{\nabla}\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}\big(\mathcal{X}^{0}\big)\otimes\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\big(\log\mathcal{X}^{0}\big)
.

It is recognized that
a TE-structure appears
as a fundamental piece of interesting structures
in various fields of mathematics.
For instance,
TE-structure is an ingredient of Frobenius manifold,
which is important
in the theory of primitive forms and flat structures due to K. Saito
[
61
]
,
the topological field theory of Dubrovin
[
18
]
,
the
tt^{\ast}
-geometry of Cecotti–Vafa
[
7
,
8
]
,
the Gromov–Witten theory,
the theory of Landau–Ginzburg models,
etc.
For the construction of Frobenius manifolds,
it is an important step to obtain TE-structures.
Abstractly,
TE-structure is also an important ingredient
of semi-infinite variation of Hodge structure
[
1
,
9
,
28
]
,
TERP structure
[
21
,
22
,
23
]
,
integrable variation of twistor structure
[
60
]
,
etc. (See also
[
50
,
53
]
.)

1.2.3
Homogeneous harmonic bundles

As Simpson applied his Kobayashi–Hitchin correspondence
to construct complex variations of Hodge structure,
we may apply Theorem
1.1

to construct TE-structures
with some additional structure.
It is done through harmonic bundles with homogeneity
as in the Hodge case.

Let
X
be a complex manifold equipped with an
S^{1}
-action.
Let
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E}\big)
be an
S^{1}
-equivariant holomorphic vector bundle.
Let
\theta
be a Higgs field of
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E}\big)
,
which is homogeneous with respect to the
S^{1}
-action,
i.e.,

t^{\ast}\theta=t^{m}\theta
for some
m\neq 0
.
Let
h
be an
S^{1}
-invariant
pluri-harmonic metric of
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta\big)
.
Then, as studied in
[
53
, Section 3]
,
we naturally obtain a TE-structure.
More strongly, it is equipped with
a grading in the sense of
[
9
,
28
]
,
and it also underlies a polarized integrable variation of pure twistor structure
of weight
0

[
60
]
.
Moreover,
if there exists an
S^{1}
-equivariant isomorphism
between

\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h\big)
and its dual,
the TE-structure is enhanced to a semi-infinite variation of
Hodge structure with a grading

[
1
,
9
,
28
]
.
If the
S^{1}
-action on
X
is trivial,
this is the same as the construction of
a variation of Hodge structure
from a Hodge bundle with a pluri-harmonic metric
for which the Hodge decomposition is orthogonal.

Let
H
be a simple normal crossing hypersurface of
X
.
From an
S^{1}
-homogeneous
good wild harmonic bundle
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h\big)

on
(X,H)
,
as mentioned above, we obtain a TE-structure with a grading
on
X\setminus H
.
Moreover, it extends to a meromorphic TE-structure on
(X,H)

as studied in
[
53
, Section 3]
. We obtain the mixed Hodge structure
as the limit objects at the boundary,
which is useful for the study of more detailed properties
of the TE-structure.

1.2.4
An equivalence

Let
X
be a complex projective manifold
with a simple normal crossing hypersurface
H

and an ample line bundle
L
,
equipped with a
{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}
-action.
A good filtered Higgs bundle

(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V},\theta)

is called
{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}
-homogeneous
if
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}
is
{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}
-equivariant
and
t^{\ast}\theta=t^{m}\cdot\theta
for some
m\neq 0
.
Then,
we obtain the following theorem by using Theorem
1.1
.
(See Section
8.1.2

for the precise definition of the stability condition
in this context.)

Theorem 1.2

(Corollary
8.11
)
.

There exists an equivalence between the following objects:

•

\mu_{L}
-polystable

{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}
-homogeneous
good filtered Higgs bundles
(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V},\theta)

on
(X,H)

satisfying

\displaystyle\int_{X}c_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V})c_{1}(L)^{\dim X-1}=\int_{X}\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V})c_{1}(L)^{\dim X-2}=0.

•

S^{1}
-homogeneous
good wild harmonic bundles on
(X,H)
.

As mentioned in Section
1.2.3
,
Theorem
1.2
allows us
to obtain a meromorphic TE-structure on
(X,H)
with a grading
from a
\mu_{L}
-polystable

{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}
-equivariant good filtered Higgs bundle
satisfying the vanishing condition. We already applied it to a classification
of solutions of the Toda equations
on
{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}

[
52
]
.
It seems natural to expect that this construction would be
another way to obtain Frobenius manifolds.

Although we explained the homogeneity with respect to
an
S^{1}
-action,
Theorem
1.2

is generalized for
K
-homogeneous good wild harmonic bundles
as explained in Section
8
,
where
K
is any compact Lie group.

2
Good filtered
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
-flat bundles and wild harmonic bundles

2.1
Filtered sheaves and filtered
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
-flat sheaves

2.1.1
Filtered sheaves

Let
X
denote a complex manifold
with a simple normal crossing hypersurface
H
.
Let
H=\bigcup_{i\in\Lambda}H_{i}

denote a decomposition
such that each
H_{i}
is smooth.
Note that
H_{i}
are not necessarily connected.
For any
P\in H
,
a holomorphic coordinate neighbourhood

(X_{P},z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
around
P

is called admissible
if
H_{P}:=H\cap X_{P}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell(P)}\{z_{i}=0\}
.
For such an admissible coordinate neighbourhood,
there exists the map

\rho_{P}\colon\{1,\ldots,\ell(P)\}\longrightarrow\Lambda

determined by
H_{\rho_{P}(i)}\cap X_{P}=\{z_{i}=0\}
. We obtain the map

\kappa_{P}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell(P)}

by
\kappa_{P}({\boldsymbol{a}})=\big(a_{\rho(1)},\ldots,a_{\rho(\ell(P))}\big)
.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)
denote the sheaf of
meromorphic functions which may have poles along
H
.
Let
\mathcal{E}
be any coherent torsion free
\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)
-module.
A filtered sheaf over
\mathcal{E}

is defined to be a tuple of coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-submodules

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{E}

({\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda})

satisfying the following conditions:

•

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\mathcal{E}

if
{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}
,
i.e.,
a_{i}\leq b_{i}
for any
i\in\Lambda
.

•

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}(\ast H)=\mathcal{E}
for any

{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}
.

•

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}+{\boldsymbol{n}}}\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\sum_{i\in\Lambda}n_{i}H_{i}\bigr)

for any
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}

and
{\boldsymbol{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Lambda}
.

•

For any
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}

there exists
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{\Lambda}

such that

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}+{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}}\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}
.

•

For any
P\!\in H
,
we take an admissible coordinate neighbourhood

(X_{P},z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
around
P
.
Then, for any
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}
,

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}_{|X_{P}}

depends only on
\kappa_{P}({\boldsymbol{a}})
.

For any coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)
-submodule
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{E}
,
we obtain a filtered sheaf

\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
over
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}

by
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
.
If
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is saturated, i.e.,

\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}:=\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is torsion-free,
we obtain a filtered sheaf

\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}
over
\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}

by
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}:=\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}\bigr)
.

A morphism of filtered sheaves

f\colon\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{2}

is defined to be a morphism

f\colon\mathcal{E}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{2}
of

\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)
-modules
such that

f(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}_{1})\subset\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}_{2}

for any
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}
.

Remark 2.1
.

The concept of filtered bundles on curves
was introduced by
Mehta and Seshadri
[
45
]

and Simpson
[
62
,
63
]
.
A higher dimensional version
was first studied by Maruyama and Yokogawa
[
42
]

for the purpose of the construction of the moduli spaces.

2.1.2
Restriction and gluing

Let
U\subset X
be any open subset. We set
H_{U}=H\cap U
.
Let
H_{U}=\bigcup_{j\in\Lambda_{U}}H_{U,j}

be the irreducible decomposition.
For any
j\in\Lambda_{U}
,
we have
i(j)\in\Lambda
such that

H_{U,j}
is a connected component of
H_{i(j)}\cap U
.
For any
P\in H_{U}
,
we set
\Lambda_{U}(P):=\{j\in\Lambda_{U}\mid P\in H_{U,j}\}
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}
be a filtered sheaf over
\mathcal{E}
. We shall define a filtered sheaf
over the
\mathcal{O}_{U}(\ast H_{U})
-module
\mathcal{E}_{|U}
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda_{U}}
.
For any
P\in H_{U}
,
we choose
{\boldsymbol{a}}(P,{\boldsymbol{b}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}

such that

a(P,{\boldsymbol{b}})_{i(j)}=b_{j}

for any
j\in\Lambda_{U}(P)
,
and we obtain the following

\mathcal{O}_{U,P}
-submodule of
the stalk
(\mathcal{E}_{|U})_{P}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}(\mathcal{E}_{|U})_{P}:=\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}(P,{\boldsymbol{b}})}(\mathcal{E})_{P}.

It is independent of the choice of

{\boldsymbol{a}}(P,{\boldsymbol{b}})
as above.
There uniquely exists
a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{U}
-submodule

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}(\mathcal{E}_{|U})

of
\mathcal{E}_{|U}

such that

(i)

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}(\mathcal{E}_{|U})(\ast H_{U})=\mathcal{E}_{|U}
,
and

(ii)
for any
P\in H_{U}
,
the stalk of
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}(\mathcal{E}_{|U})
at
P
is
equal to
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}(\mathcal{E}_{|U})_{P}
.
Thus, we obtain a filtered sheaf

\mathcal{P}_{\ast}(\mathcal{E}_{|U})
over
\mathcal{E}_{|U}
,
which is denoted as

\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{|U}
.

Let
X=\bigcup_{k\in\Gamma}X^{(k)}
be an open covering. We set
H^{(k)}=H\cap X^{(k)}
.
For any filtered sheaf
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}
over
\mathcal{E}
,
we obtain
filtered sheaves

\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{|X^{(k)}}

over
\mathcal{E}_{|X^{(k)}}

as the restriction.
Conversely,
let
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\big(\mathcal{E}_{|X^{(k)}}\big)

(k\in\Gamma)

be filtered sheaves over
\mathcal{E}_{|X^{(k)}}

such that

\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\big(\mathcal{E}_{|X^{(k)}}\big)_{|X^{(k)}\cap X^{(\ell)}}=\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\big(\mathcal{E}_{|X^{(\ell)}}\big)_{|X^{(k)}\cap X^{(\ell)}}

for any
k,\ell\in\Gamma
.

Lemma 2.2
.

There uniquely exists
a filtered sheaf
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}
over
\mathcal{E}

such that

\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{|X^{(k)}}=\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\big(\mathcal{E}_{|X^{(k)}}\big)

for any
k\in\Gamma
.

Proof.

Let
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\Lambda
.
For any
P\in H
,
there exists
k\in\Gamma

such that
P\in X^{(k)}
.
Let
H^{(k)}=\bigcup_{j\in\Lambda^{(k)}}H^{(k)}_{j}

be the irreducible decomposition.
For any
j\in\Lambda^{(k)}
,
we have
i(k,j)\in\Lambda
such that
H^{(k)}_{j}
is a connected component of

H_{i(k,j)}\cap X^{(k)}
.
Thus, we obtain a map

\Lambda^{(k)}\longrightarrow\Lambda
.
For any
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}
,
let
{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(k)}
be the image of
{\boldsymbol{a}}

by the induced map

{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda^{(k)}}
,
and we obtain the following

\mathcal{O}_{X,P}
-submodule of
\mathcal{E}_{P}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\mathcal{E})_{P}:=\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(k)}}\big(\mathcal{E}_{|X^{(k)}}\big)_{P}.

There uniquely exists a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-submodule

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{E}

such that

(i)

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}(\ast H)=\mathcal{E}
,
and
(ii)
for any
P\in H
,
the stalk of
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\mathcal{E})
at
P

is equal to
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\mathcal{E})_{P}
.
Thus, we obtain a filtered sheaf
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}
over
\mathcal{E}

with the desired property.
The uniqueness is also clear.
∎

2.1.3
Reflexive filtered sheaves

A filtered sheaf
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}
on
(X,H)

is called reflexive
if each
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}
is a reflexive
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module.
Note that it is equivalent to
the “reflexive and saturated” condition
in
[
46
, Definition 3.17]

by the following lemma.

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose that

\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}
is reflexive.
Let
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}
. We take
a_{i}-1<b\leq a_{i}
,
and let
{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}

be determined by

a_{j}^{\prime}=a_{j}

(j\neq i)
and
a^{\prime}_{i}=b
.
Then,

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}

is a torsion-free
\mathcal{O}_{H_{i}}
-module.

Proof.

Let
s
be a section of

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}

on an open set
U\subset D_{i}
.
There exists an open subset
\widetilde{U}\subset X

and a section
\widetilde{s}
of
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}
on
\widetilde{U}

such that
\widetilde{U}\cap D_{i}=U

and that
\widetilde{s}
induces
s
.
Note that there exists
Z\subset\widetilde{U}

of codimension
2

such that
\widetilde{s}_{|\widetilde{U}\setminus Z}

is a section of

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}_{|\widetilde{U}\setminus Z}
.
Because
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}
is reflexive,
there exists a section
\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
of

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}
on
\widetilde{U}

such that

\widetilde{s}^{\prime}_{|\widetilde{U}\setminus Z}=\widetilde{s}_{|\widetilde{U}\setminus Z}
.
Hence, we obtain that
\widetilde{s}
is a section of

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}
,
i.e.,
s=0
.
∎

The following lemma is clear.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}
be a reflexive filtered sheaf on
(X,H)
.
Then a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)
-submodule
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{E}

is saturated if and only if
the induced filtered sheaf
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is reflexive.

2.1.4
Filtered
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
-flat sheaves

Let
\lambda
be any complex number.
Let
\mathcal{E}
be a coherent torsion-free
\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)
-module.
A
\lambda
-connection

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\colon\mathcal{E}\longrightarrow\Omega_{X}^{1}\otimes\mathcal{E}

is a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear morphism of sheaves such that

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}(fs)=f\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}(s)+\lambda{\rm d}f\otimes s

for any local sections
f
and
s

of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
and
\mathcal{E}
, respectively.
Note that
an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-morphism

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\circ\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\colon\mathcal{E}\longrightarrow\Omega_{X}^{2}\otimes\mathcal{E}
is induced.
If
\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\circ\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}=0
,
it is called a flat
\lambda
-connection.
When
\mathcal{E}
is equipped with a flat
\lambda
-connection,
a
\lambda
-flat subsheaf of
\mathcal{E}

means a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-submodule
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{E}

such that

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\subset\Omega_{X}^{1}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
.
A pair of a filtered sheaf
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}
over
\mathcal{E}

and a flat
\lambda
-connection

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}
of
\mathcal{E}
is called
a filtered
\lambda
-flat connection.
It is called reflexive
if
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}
is reflexive.

2.2
\boldsymbol{\mu_{L}}
-stability condition for filtered
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
-flat sheaves

Let
X
be a connected projective manifold with a simple normal crossing
hypersurface
H=\bigcup_{i\in\Lambda}H_{i}
.
Let
L
be an ample line bundle.

2.2.1
Slope of filtered sheaves

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}
be a filtered sheaf on
(X,H)
.
Recall the definition of
the parabolic first Chern class
c_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E})
.
Let
\eta_{i}
be the generic point of
H_{i}
.
Note that

\mathcal{O}_{X,\eta_{i}}
-modules

(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E})_{\eta_{i}}

depends only on
a_{i}
,
which is denoted by
\mathcal{P}_{a_{i}}(\mathcal{E}_{\eta_{i}})
. We obtain
\mathcal{O}_{H_{i},\eta_{i}}
-modules

\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{\mathcal{P}}_{a}(\mathcal{E}_{\eta_{i}}):=\mathcal{P}_{a}(\mathcal{E}_{\eta_{i}})\big/\mathcal{P}_{<a}(\mathcal{E}_{\eta_{i}})
.
Then, we set

\displaystyle c_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}):=c_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E})-\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\sum_{a_{i}-1<a\leq a_{i}}a\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{\mathcal{P}}_{a}(\mathcal{E}_{\eta_{i}})[H_{i}]\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{R}}).

(2.1)

Here,
[H_{i}]
denote the cohomology class induced by

H_{i}
.
It is easy to see that

c_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V})

is independent of the choice of
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}
. We set

\displaystyle\mu_{L}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}):=\frac{1}{\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits\mathcal{E}}\int_{X}c_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E})\cdot c_{1}(L)^{n-1}.

It is called the slope of
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}

with respect to
L
.
The following is proved in

[
46
, Lemma 3.7]
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{(1)}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{(2)}

be a morphism of filtered sheaves
which is generically an isomorphism,
i.e.,
the induced morphism

\mathcal{E}^{(1)}_{\eta(X)}\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}^{(2)}_{\eta(X)}

at the generic point of
X
is an isomorphism.
Then,
\mu_{L}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{(1)}\big)\leq\mu_{L}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{(2)}\big)
holds.
If the equality holds,

f
is an isomorphism in codimension one,
i.e.,
there exists an algebraic subset
Z\subset X

such that

(i)
the codimension of
Z
is larger than
2
,

(ii)

f_{|X\setminus Z}\colon\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{(1)}_{|X\setminus Z}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{(2)}_{|X\setminus Z}

is an isomorphism.

2.2.2
\boldsymbol{\mu_{L}}
-stability condition

A filtered
\lambda
-flat sheaf

(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda})
on
(X,H)

is called
\mu_{L}
-stable
(resp.
\mu_{L}
-semistable)
if the following holds:

•

Let
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{E}

be any
\lambda
-flat
\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)
-submodule
such that

0<\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})<\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits(\mathcal{E})
.
Then,

\mu_{L}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{\prime})<\mu_{L}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E})

(resp.
\mu_{L}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\leq\mu_{L}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E})
)
holds.

A filtered
\lambda
-flat sheaf

\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)

is called
\mu_{L}
-polystable
if the following holds:

•

\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)
is
\mu_{L}
-semistable.

•

\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)=\bigoplus\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{i},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{i}\big)
,
where each
\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{i},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{i}\big)

is
\mu_{L}
-stable.

The following is standard.
(See
[
46
, Section 3.1.3]
and
[
49
, Section 2.1.4]
.)

Lemma 2.6
.

Suppose that

(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda})
is
a
\mu_{L}
-polystable reflexive filtered
\lambda
-flat sheaf.
Then, there exists a unique decomposition

\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{i},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{i}\big)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m(i)}

such that

(i)

\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{i},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{i}\big)

are
\mu_{L}
-stable,

(ii)

\mu_{L}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{i})=\mu_{L}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E})
,

(iii)

\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{i},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{i}\big)\not\simeq\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{j},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{j}\big)

(i\neq j)
.

Remark 2.7
.

In
[
46
, Section 3.1.3]
,
“the inequality

\mathop{\rm par\textrm{-}deg}\nolimits_{L}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\ast})<\mathop{\rm par\textrm{-}deg}\nolimits_{L}(\mathcal{E}_{\ast})
”
should be corrected to
“the inequality

\mu_{L}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\ast})<\mu_{L}(\mathcal{E}_{\ast})
”.

2.3
Filtered bundles

2.3.1
Filtered bundles in the local case

We recall the notion of filtered bundle
in the local case. We shall explain it in the global case
in Section
2.3.3
.
Let
U
be a neighbourhood of

(0,\ldots,0)
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. We set
H_{U,i}:=U\cap\{z_{i}=0\}
,
and

H_{U}:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}H_{U,i}

for some
0\leq\ell\leq n
.
Let
\mathcal{V}
be a locally free

\mathcal{O}_{U}(\ast H_{U})
-module.
A filtered bundle

\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}
over
\mathcal{V}

is a tuple of locally free
\mathcal{O}_{U}
-submodules

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}

({\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell})

such that the following holds:

•

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\mathcal{V}

if
{\boldsymbol{a}}\leq{\boldsymbol{b}}
,
i.e.,
a_{i}\leq b_{i}
for any
i=1,\ldots,\ell
.

•

There exists a frame

{\boldsymbol{v}}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})

of
\mathcal{V}

and tuples
{\boldsymbol{a}}(v_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}

(j=1,\ldots,r)

such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\mathcal{V}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{r}\mathcal{O}_{U}\bigg(\sum_{i}\bigl[b_{i}-a_{i}(v_{j})\bigr]H_{U,i}\bigg)\cdot v_{j},

(2.2)

where we set
[c]:=\max\{p\in{\mathbb{Z}}\mid p\leq c\}

for any
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Clearly,
a filtered bundle over
\mathcal{V}

is a filtered sheaf over
\mathcal{V}
.

Remark 2.8
.

We set
\mathcal{R}:={\mathbb{C}}[\![z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]\!]

and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}:=\mathcal{R}\big[z_{1}^{-1},\ldots,z_{\ell}^{-1}\big]
.
For a free
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
-module
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
,
a filtered bundle over
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}

is defined to be a tuple
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\widehat{\mathcal{V}}:=\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\widehat{\mathcal{V}}\mid{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell(P)}\bigr)

of free
\mathcal{R}
-submodules
satisfying similar conditions as above.

2.3.2
Pull back, push-forward and descent with respect to ramified coverings

in the local case

Let
\varphi\colon{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{n}

be given by

\varphi(\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n})=\big(\zeta_{1}^{m_{1}},\ldots,\zeta_{\ell}^{m_{\ell}},\zeta_{\ell+1},\ldots,\zeta_{n}\big)
. We set
U^{\prime}:=\varphi^{-1}(U)
,

H_{U^{\prime},i}:=\varphi^{-1}(H_{U,i})

and
H_{U^{\prime}}:=\varphi^{-1}(H_{U})
.
The induced ramified covering

U^{\prime}\longrightarrow U
is also denoted by
\varphi
.

For any
{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
,
we set

\varphi^{\ast}({\boldsymbol{b}})=(m_{i}b_{i})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
.
For any filtered bundle
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}_{1}
on

(U,H_{U})
,
we define a filtered bundle
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{1}

on
(U^{\prime},H_{U^{\prime}})
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{1}=\sum_{\varphi^{\ast}({\boldsymbol{b}})+{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq{\boldsymbol{a}}}\varphi^{\ast}\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\mathcal{V}_{1}\bigr)\Bigl(\sum n_{i}H_{U^{\prime},i}\Bigr).

We set
\varphi^{\ast}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}_{1}):=\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{1}
.
Thus,
we obtain the pull back functor
\varphi^{\ast}

from the category of filtered bundles on
(U,H_{U})

to the category of filtered bundles on
(U^{\prime},H_{U^{\prime}})
.

For any
{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
,
we set
\varphi_{\ast}({\boldsymbol{b}})=(m_{i}^{-1}b_{i})
.
For any filtered bundle
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}_{2}
on
(U^{\prime},H_{U^{\prime}})
,
we obtain the following filtered bundle

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\varphi_{\ast}(\mathcal{V}_{2}):=\varphi_{\ast}\mathcal{P}_{\varphi_{\ast}({\boldsymbol{b}})}\mathcal{V}_{2}.

In this way, we obtain a functor
\varphi_{\ast}

from the category of filtered bundles on
(U^{\prime},H_{U^{\prime}})

to the category of filtered bundles on
(U,H_{U})
.

We set
G:=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\big\{\mu_{i}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}\mid\mu_{i}^{m_{i}}=1\big\}
. We define the action of
G
on
U^{\prime}

by

\displaystyle(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{\ell})(\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n})=(\mu_{1}\zeta_{1},\ldots,\mu_{\ell}\zeta_{\ell},\zeta_{\ell+1},\ldots,\zeta_{n}).

We identify
G
as the Galois group of the ramified covering

U^{\prime}\longrightarrow U
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}_{3}
be a
G
-equivariant
filtered bundles on
(U^{\prime},H_{U^{\prime}})
.
Then,

\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\varphi_{\ast}\mathcal{V}_{3}

is equipped with an induced
G
-action. We obtain a filtered bundle

(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\varphi_{\ast}\mathcal{V}_{3})^{G}

on
(U,H_{U})

as the
G
-invariant part of
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\varphi_{\ast}\mathcal{V}_{3}
,
which is called the descent of
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}_{3}

with respect to the
G
-action. In this way,
we obtain a functor
from the category of
G
-equivariant
filtered bundles on
(U^{\prime},H_{U^{\prime}})

to the category of filtered bundles on
(U,H_{U})
.

For a filtered bundle
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}_{1}
on
(U,H_{U})
,
the pull back

\varphi^{\ast}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}_{1})
is
a
G
-equivariant filtered bundle on
(U^{\prime},H_{U^{\prime}})
,
and its descent is naturally isomorphic to

\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}_{1}
.

2.3.3
Filtered bundles in the global case

We use the notation in Section
2.1.1
.
Let
\mathcal{V}
be a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)
-module.
A filtered bundle

\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}=\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}\mid{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}\bigr)
over
\mathcal{V}

be a sequence of locally free

\mathcal{O}_{X}
-submodules

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{V}

such that the following holds:

•

For any
P\!\in H
,
we take an admissible coordinate neighbourhood
(X_{P},z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})

around
P
.
Then, for any
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}
,

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}_{|X_{P}}

depends only on
\kappa_{P}({\boldsymbol{a}})
,
denoted as

\mathcal{P}^{(P)}_{\kappa_{P}({\boldsymbol{a}})}(\mathcal{V}_{|X_{P}})
.

•

The sequence

\big(\mathcal{P}^{(P)}_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}(\mathcal{V}_{|X_{P}})\mid{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell(P)}\big)

is a filtered bundle over
\mathcal{V}_{|X_{P}}

in the sense of Section
2.3.1
.

In other words,
a filtered bundle is a filtered sheaf
(see Section
2.1.1
)
satisfying the condition in Section
2.3.1

locally around any point of
H
.

Remark 2.9
.

The higher dimensional version of filtered bundles
was introduced in
[
47
,
48
]

with a different formulation.
See also
[
5
,
6
]
. In this paper, we essentially follow Iyer and Simpson
[
29
]
.

2.3.4
The induced bundles and filtrations

For any
I\subset\Lambda
,
let
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{I}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}

be the element
whose
j
-th component is
0

(j\in\Lambda\setminus I)

or
1

(j\in I)
. We also set
H_{I}:=\bigcap_{i\in I}H_{i}

and

\partial H_{I}:=H_{I}\cap\bigl(\bigcup_{j\in\Lambda\setminus I}H_{j}\bigr)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}
be a filtered bundle on
(X,H)
.
Take
i\in\Lambda
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}
.
For any
a_{i}-1\leq b\leq a_{i}
,
we set
{\boldsymbol{a}}(b,i):={\boldsymbol{a}}+(b-a_{i}){\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{i}
. We set

\displaystyle{}^{i}\!F_{b}\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\mathcal{V})_{|H_{i}}\bigr):=\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}(b,i)}\mathcal{V}/\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}(a_{i}-1,i)}\mathcal{V}.

It is naturally regarded as
a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{H_{i}}
-module.
Moreover, it is a subbundle of

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\mathcal{V})_{|H_{i}}
. In this way,
we obtain a filtration

{}^{i}\!F
of

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(\mathcal{V})_{|H_{i}}

indexed by

]a_{i}-1,a_{i}]
. We shall also denote it as just
F

if there is no risk of confusion.

We obtain the induced filtrations

{}^{i}\!F

of
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}_{|H_{I}}

if
i\in I
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{a}}_{I}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{I}
denote the image of
{\boldsymbol{a}}

by the projection
{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{I}
.
Set
]{\boldsymbol{a}}_{I}-{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{I},{\boldsymbol{a}}_{I}]:=\prod_{i\in I}]a_{i}-1,a_{i}]
.
For any
{\boldsymbol{b}}\in]{\boldsymbol{a}}_{I}-{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{I},{\boldsymbol{a}}_{I}]
,
we set

\displaystyle{}^{I}\!F_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\big(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}_{|H_{I}}\big):=\bigcap_{i\in I}{}^{i}\!F_{b_{i}}\big(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}_{|H_{I}}\big).

By the condition of filtered bundles,
the following compatibility condition holds.

•

Let
P
be any point of
H_{I}
.
There exist a neighbourhood
X_{P}
of
P
in
X

and a non-canonical decomposition

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}_{|X_{P}\cap H_{I}}=\bigoplus_{{\boldsymbol{b}}\in]{\boldsymbol{a}}_{I}-{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{I},{\boldsymbol{a}}_{I}]}\mathcal{G}_{P,{\boldsymbol{b}}}

such that the following holds
for any
{\boldsymbol{c}}\in]{\boldsymbol{a}}_{I}-{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{I},{\boldsymbol{a}}_{I}]
:

\displaystyle{}^{I}\!F_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}_{|H_{I}\cap X_{P}})=\bigoplus_{{\boldsymbol{b}}\leq{\boldsymbol{c}}}\mathcal{G}_{P,{\boldsymbol{b}}}.

(2.3)

Indeed, there exists a frame
{\boldsymbol{v}}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})

of
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}
around
P

with tuples
{\boldsymbol{a}}(v_{i})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell(P)}
of real numbers
satisfying (
2.2
),
where
{\boldsymbol{b}}
is replaced with
{\boldsymbol{a}}
.
There exists the bijection

\kappa\colon I\simeq\{1,\ldots,\ell(P)\}

determined by
H_{i}\cap X_{P}=\{z_{\kappa(i)}=0\}
,
by which we identify
I
with
\{1,\ldots,\ell(P)\}
.
Let
\mathcal{G}_{P,{\boldsymbol{b}}}

be the subbundle of

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}_{|X_{P}\cap H_{I}}

generated by
v_{j|X_{P}\cap H_{I}}

satisfying

{\boldsymbol{a}}(v_{j})={\boldsymbol{b}}
.
Then, we obtain the decomposition (
2.3
).

For any
{\boldsymbol{c}}\in]{\boldsymbol{a}}_{I}-{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{I},{\boldsymbol{a}}_{I}]
,
we obtain the following locally free
\mathcal{O}_{H_{I}}
-modules:

\displaystyle{}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}):=\frac{{}^{I}\!F_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}\big(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}_{|H_{I}}\big)}{\sum_{{\boldsymbol{b}}\lneq{\boldsymbol{c}}}{}^{I}\!F_{{\boldsymbol{b}}}\big(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}_{|H_{I}}\big)}.

Here,
{\boldsymbol{b}}=(b_{i})\lneq{\boldsymbol{c}}=(c_{i})
means
that
b_{i}\leq c_{i}
for any
i

and that
{\boldsymbol{b}}\neq{\boldsymbol{c}}
.
Clearly,
if
{\boldsymbol{c}}\in]{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\prime}_{I}-{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{I},{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\prime}_{I}]

and
{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\prime}_{\Lambda\setminus I}={\boldsymbol{a}}_{\Lambda\setminus I}
,
we obtain

{}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V})={}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\prime}}\mathcal{V})
.

2.3.5
The induced filtered bundles

For
{\boldsymbol{c}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{I}
,
we choose

{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}

such that
{\boldsymbol{c}}\in]{\boldsymbol{a}}_{I}-{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{I},{\boldsymbol{a}}_{I}]
,
and we obtain the following

\mathcal{O}_{H_{I}}(\ast\partial H_{I})
-module:

\displaystyle{}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{V}):={}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V})(\ast\partial H_{I}).

It is independent of the choice of
{\boldsymbol{a}}
as above. We obtain the irreducible decomposition

\partial H_{I}=\bigcup_{i\in\Lambda(I)}H_{I,i}
.
For any
i\in\Lambda(I)
,
there exists
j(i)\in\Lambda\setminus I

such that

H_{I,i}
is a connected component of
H_{I}\cap H_{j(i)}
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{d}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda(I)}
.
For
P\in\partial H_{I}
,
there exists
{\boldsymbol{a}}({\boldsymbol{c}},{\boldsymbol{d}},P)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}

such that

(i)

a({\boldsymbol{c}},{\boldsymbol{d}},P)_{j}=c_{j}

(j\in I)
,

(ii)

a({\boldsymbol{c}},{\boldsymbol{d}},P)_{j(i)}=d_{i}

(i\in\Lambda(I),\,P\in H_{I,i})
. We obtain an
\mathcal{O}_{X,P}
-submodule

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\bigl({}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{V})\bigr)_{P}:={}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}({\boldsymbol{c}},{\boldsymbol{d}},P)}\mathcal{V}\bigl)_{P}\subset{}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{V})_{P}.

Note that

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\bigl({}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{V})\bigr)_{P}

is independent of the choice of
{\boldsymbol{a}}(P)
.
There uniquely exists
an
\mathcal{O}_{H_{I}}
-submodule

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\bigl({}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{V})\bigr)\subset{}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{V})

whose stalk at
P

(P\in\partial H_{I})

are equal to

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\bigl({}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{V})\bigr)_{P}
.
Thus, we obtain the following filtered bundle
over
{}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{V})

on
(H_{I},\partial H_{I})
:

\displaystyle{}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}):=\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\bigl({}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{V})\bigr)\mid{\boldsymbol{d}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda(I)}\bigr).

2.3.6
First and second Chern characters for filtered bundles

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}

be a filtered bundle over
(X,H)
.
Take any
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}
.
As recalled in Section
2.2.1
,
we obtain the parabolic first Chern class:

\displaystyle c_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V})=c_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V})-\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\sum_{a_{i}-1<b\leq a_{i}}a_{i}\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits{}^{i}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{b}(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{E}_{|H_{i}})\cdot[H_{i}]\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{R}}).

To explain the second parabolic Chern character in
H^{4}(X,{\mathbb{R}})
,
let us introduce some notation.
Let
\mathop{\rm Irr}\nolimits(H_{i}\cap H_{j})
be the set of the irreducible components
of
H_{i}\cap H_{j}
.
For
C\in\mathop{\rm Irr}\nolimits(H_{I})
,
let

[C]\in H^{4}(X,{\mathbb{R}})
denotes the induced cohomology class,
and let
{}^{C}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V})

denote the restriction of

{}^{I}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}}(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V})

to
C
.
Moreover,

\iota_{i\ast}\colon H^{2}(H_{i},{\mathbb{R}})\longrightarrow H^{4}(X,{\mathbb{R}})

denotes the Gysin map
induced by
\iota_{i}\colon H_{i}\longrightarrow X
.
Then, the second parabolic Chern character is given as follows.

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}):={}

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V})-\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\sum_{a_{i}-1<b\leq a_{i}}b\cdot\iota_{i\ast}\bigl(c_{1}\bigl({}^{i}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{b}\big(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}_{|H_{i}}\big)\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\sum_{a_{i}-1<b\leq a_{i}}b^{2}\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits\big({}^{i}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{b}(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V})\big)\cdot[H_{i}]^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,j)\in\Lambda^{2}\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\sum_{C\in\mathop{\rm Irr}\nolimits(H_{i}\cap H_{j})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{i}-1<c_{i}\leq a_{i}\\
a_{j}-1<c_{j}\leq a_{j}\end{subarray}}c_{i}\cdot c_{j}\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits{}^{C}\!\mathop{\rm Gr}\nolimits^{F}_{(c_{i},c_{j})}(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V})\cdot[C].

Remark 2.10
.

The higher Chern character
for filtered sheaves was defined by
Iyer and Simpson
[
29
]

in a systematic way. In this paper, we adopt the definition
of
\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V})

in
[
46
]
.

2.4
Good filtered
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
-flat bundles

Let
X
be a complex manifold with a simple normal crossing
hypersurface
H=\bigcup_{i\in\Lambda}H_{i}
.

2.4.1
Good set of irregular values at
\boldsymbol{P}

Let
P
be any point of
H
. We take an admissible holomorphic coordinate neighbourhood

(X_{P},z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
around
P
.
Let
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)_{P}
. If
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X,P}
,
we set
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(f):=(0,\ldots,0)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell(P)}
. If there exists

{\boldsymbol{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\leq 0}^{\ell(P)}\setminus\{(0,\ldots,0)\}

such that
(i)

g:=f\prod z_{i}^{-n_{i}}\in\mathcal{O}_{X,P}
,

(ii)

g(P)\neq 0
, then we set
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(f):={\boldsymbol{n}}
. Otherwise,

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(f)
is not defined.

For any
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)_{P}/\mathcal{O}_{X,P}
,
we take a lift
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)_{P}
.
If
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}})
is defined,
we set

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\mathfrak{a}):=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}})
.
Otherwise,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\mathfrak{a})
is not defined.
Note that it is independent of the choice of
a lift
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{P}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)_{P}/\mathcal{O}_{X,P}

be a finite subset. We say that
\mathcal{I}_{P}
is a good set of irregular values
if the following conditions are satisfied:

•

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\mathfrak{a})
is defined for any
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{I}_{P}
.

•

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b})
is defined for any

\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{I}_{P}
.

•

\{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b})\mid\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{I}_{P}\}

is totally ordered
with respect to
the order
\leq_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell(P)}}
.
Here, we define

{\boldsymbol{n}}\leq_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell(P)}}{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\prime}

if
n_{i}\leq n_{i}^{\prime}
for any
i
.

2.4.2
Good filtered
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
-flat bundles

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)
-module
with a flat
\lambda
-connection.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}
be a filtered bundle over
\mathcal{V}
.
For any
P\in X
,
let
\mathcal{O}_{X,\widehat{P}}
denote the completion of
the local ring
\mathcal{O}_{X,P}

with respect to the maximal ideal.
Note that Remark
2.8

has a natural generalization
to filtered
\lambda
-flat bundles. We say that
\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)

is unramifiedly good at
P
if the following holds:

•

There exist a good set of irregular values

\mathcal{I}_{P}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)_{P}/\mathcal{O}_{X,P}

and a decomposition of filtered
\lambda
-flat bundles

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X,\widehat{P}}=\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{I}_{P}}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{a}},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\mathfrak{a}}\big)

(2.4)

such that

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\mathfrak{a}}-{\rm d}\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{a}}}

are logarithmic with respect to the lattices

\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{a}}

for any
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell(P)}

and
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{I}_{P}
,
i.e.,

\displaystyle\big(\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{\mathfrak{a}}-{\rm d}\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big)\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{a}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{a}}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{X}(\log H).

(2.5)

Here,

\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
denote lifts of
\mathfrak{a}

to
\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)_{P}
.

We say that
\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)

is good at
P
if the following holds:

•

There exist a neighbourhood
X_{P}
of
P
in
X

and a covering map
\varphi_{P}\colon X_{P}^{\prime}\longrightarrow X_{P}

ramified over
H_{P}=H\cap X_{P}

such that

\varphi_{P}^{\ast}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)

is unramifiedly good at
\varphi_{P}^{-1}(P)
.
(See Section
2.3.2

for the pull back of filtered bundles.)

We say that

\big(\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)

is good (resp. unramifiedly good)
if it is good (resp. unramifiedly good)
at any point of
H
.

2.5
Prolongation of holomorphic vector bundles
with a Hermitian metric

Let
X
be any complex manifold
with a simple normal crossing hypersurface

H=\bigcup_{i\in\Lambda}H_{i}
.
Let
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E}\big)
be a holomorphic vector bundle
on
X\setminus H

with a Hermitian metric
h
.
Let us recall
the construction of

\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)
-module
\mathcal{P}^{h}E

and
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules
\mathcal{P}^{h}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}E

({\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda})
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}
.
For any open subset
\mathcal{U}\subset X
,
let
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{h}E(\mathcal{U})

be the space of holomorphic sections
s
of

E_{|\mathcal{U}\setminus H}

satisfying the following condition:

•

For any point
P
of
\mathcal{U}\cap H
,
let
(X_{P},z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})

be an admissible holomorphic coordinate neighbourhood
around
P

such that
X_{P}
is relatively compact in
\mathcal{U}
.
Set
{\boldsymbol{c}}=\kappa_{P}({\boldsymbol{a}})
.
(See Section
2.1.1
.)
Then,

\displaystyle\bigl|s\bigr|_{h}=O\Biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{\ell(P)}|z_{i}|^{-c_{i}-\epsilon}\Biggr)

holds on
X_{P}\setminus H
for any
\epsilon>0
.

We obtain an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module

\mathcal{P}^{h}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}E
. We set

\mathcal{P}^{h}E:=\bigcup_{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\Lambda}}\mathcal{P}^{h}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}E

which is an
\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)
-module.
Note that in general,

\mathcal{P}^{h}_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}E

are not necessarily coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}
be a filtered bundle over
(X,H)
.
Let
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E}\big)
be the holomorphic vector bundle
obtained as the restriction of
\mathcal{V}
to
X\setminus H
.
A Hermitian metric
h
is called adapted to

\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}

if
\mathcal{P}^{h}_{\ast}E=\mathcal{P}_{\ast}\mathcal{V}

in
\iota_{\ast}(E)=\iota_{\ast}\big(\mathcal{V}_{|X\setminus H}\big)
,
where
\iota\colon X\setminus H\longrightarrow X
denotes
the inclusion.

2.5.1
A sufficient condition

We mention a useful sufficient condition
for
\mathcal{P}^{h}_{\ast}E
to be a filtered bundle,
although we do not use it in this paper.
Let
g_{X\setminus H}
be a Kähler metric
satisfying the following condition

[
12
]
:

•

For any
P\in H
,
take an admissible holomorphic coordinate neighbourhood

(X_{P},z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
around
P

such that

X_{P}
is isomorphic to

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\{|z_{i}|<1\}

by the coordinate system.
Set
X_{P}^{\prime}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\{|z_{i}|<1/2\}
.
Then,
g_{|X_{P}^{\prime}\setminus H}
is mutually bounded
with the restriction of the Poincaré metric

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\ell(P)}\frac{{\rm d}z_{i}\,{\rm d}\overline{z}_{i}}{|z_{i}|^{2}(\log|z_{i}|^{2})^{2}}+\sum_{i=\ell(P)+1}^{n}{\rm d}z_{i}\,{\rm d}\overline{z}_{i}.

A Hermitian metric
h
of
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E}\big)
is called acceptable
if the curvature of the Chern connection
is bounded with respect to
h
and
g_{X\setminus H}
.
The following theorem is
proved in

[
51
, Theorem 21.3.1]
.

Theorem 2.12
.

If
h
is acceptable,
then
\mathcal{P}^{h}_{\ast}E
is a filtered bundle,
and
\mathcal{P}^{h}E
is a locally free

\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast H)
-module.

2.6
Harmonic bundles

2.6.1
Pluri-harmonic metrics for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
-flat bundles

Let
Y
be any complex manifold.
Let
E
be a
C^{\infty}
-vector bundle on
Y
.
Let
A^{p,q}(E)
denote the space of

C^{\infty}
-sections of

\Omega^{p,q}\otimes E
. We set
A^{\ell}(E):=\bigoplus_{p+q=\ell}A^{p,q}(E)
. In this context,
a
\lambda
-connection of
E

is a differential operator

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\colon A^{0}(E)\longrightarrow A^{1}(E)

such that

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}(fs)=f\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}(s)+\big(\lambda\partial_{Y}+\overline{\partial}_{Y}\big)f\otimes s

for any

f\in C^{\infty}(Y)
and
s\in A^{0}(E)
. We obtain a section

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\circ\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\in A^{2}(\mathop{\rm End}\nolimits(E))
.
A
\lambda
-connection is called flat
if
\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\circ\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}=0
.

Let
\big(E,\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)
be a
\lambda
-flat bundle on
Y
. We decompose
\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}={\rm d}_{E}^{\prime\prime}+{\rm d}_{E}^{\prime}
into the
(0,1)
-part
and the
(1,0)
-part.
Then,
\big(E,{\rm d}_{E}^{\prime\prime}\big)
is a holomorphic vector bundle.
Let
h
be a Hermitian metric of
E
.
From
h
and
{\rm d}^{\prime\prime}_{E}
,
we obtain the differential operator
\delta^{\prime}_{E,h}

such that

{\rm d}^{\prime\prime}_{E}+\delta^{\prime}_{E,h}
is a Chern connection.
From
{\rm d}^{\prime}_{E}
and
h
,
we obtain the
(0,1)
-operator
\delta^{\prime\prime}_{E,h}

determined by

\lambda\partial h(u,v)=h({\rm d}^{\prime}_{E}u,v)+h(u,\delta^{\prime\prime}_{E,h}v)
.
As in
[
49
, Section 2.2.1]
,
we obtain the operators

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}_{E,h}:=\frac{1}{1+|\lambda|^{2}}\big({\rm d}^{\prime\prime}_{E}+\lambda\delta^{\prime}_{E,h}\big),\qquad\partial_{E,h}:=\frac{1}{1+|\lambda|^{2}}\big(\overline{\lambda}{\rm d}^{\prime}_{E}+\delta^{\prime}_{E,h}\big),

\displaystyle\theta^{\dagger}_{E,h}:=\frac{1}{1+|\lambda|^{2}}\big(\overline{\lambda}{\rm d}^{\prime\prime}_{E}-\delta^{\prime\prime}_{E,h}\big),\qquad\theta_{E,h}:=\frac{1}{1+|\lambda|^{2}}\big({\rm d}^{\prime}_{E}-\lambda\partial^{\prime}_{E,h}\big).

Note that

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}=\overline{\partial}_{E,h}+\theta_{E,h}+\lambda\big(\partial_{E,h}+\theta^{\dagger}_{E,h}\big)
. We set

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda\star}_{E,h}:=\delta^{\prime}_{E,h}-\delta^{\prime\prime}_{E,h}=\partial_{E,h}+\theta_{E,h}^{\dagger}\allowbreak-\overline{\lambda}\big(\overline{\partial}_{E,h}+\theta_{E,h}\big)
,
and
G(h):=\bigl[\mathbb{D}^{\lambda},\mathbb{D}^{\lambda\star}_{E,h}\bigr]
.

Definition 2.13
.

h
is called a pluri-harmonic metric of
\big(E,\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)

if
G(h)=0
. Such a tuple
\big(E,\mathbb{D}^{\lambda},h\big)
is called a harmonic bundle.

If
\lambda\neq 0
, because

\big(1+|\lambda|^{2}\big)\big(\overline{\partial}_{E,h}+\theta_{E,h}\big)=\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}-\lambda\mathbb{D}_{E,h}^{\lambda\star}
,
and
\big(\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)^{2}=\big(\mathbb{D}_{E,h}^{\lambda\star}\big)^{2}=0
,
we obtain

\displaystyle G(h)=-\frac{(1+|\lambda|^{2})^{2}}{\lambda}\big(\overline{\partial}_{E,h}+\theta_{E,h}\big)^{2}=-\frac{(1+|\lambda|^{2})^{2}}{\lambda}\bigl(\overline{\partial}_{E,h}^{2}+\overline{\partial}_{E,h}\theta_{E,h}+\theta_{E,h}^{2}\bigr).

Hence,

G(h)=0
implies that

\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E,h},\theta_{E,h}\big)
is a Higgs bundle.
The metric
h
is a pluri-harmonic metric for

\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E,h},\theta_{E,h}\big)
.
Conversely,
if
h
is a pluri-harmonic metric for
a Higgs bundle
\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta\big)
,
we obtain
the Chern connection

\overline{\partial}_{E}+\partial_{E,h}

associated with
\overline{\partial}_{E}
and
h
,
and the adjoint
\theta^{\dagger}_{h}

of
\theta
with respect to
h
. We obtain a flat
\lambda
-connection

\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{h}=\overline{\partial}_{E}+\lambda\theta_{h}+\lambda\partial_{E,h}+\theta
.
The metric
h
is a pluri-harmonic metric
for
(E,\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}_{h})
.

Remark 2.14
.

If
\lambda=0
,
a flat
0
-connection is equivalent to
a Higgs bundle

\big(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta\big)

by the relation
\mathbb{D}^{1}=\overline{\partial}_{E}+\theta
. In this case,
we obtain

G(h)=\bigl[\overline{\partial}_{E}+\theta,\partial_{E,h}+\theta_{h}^{\dagger}\bigr]
,
and hence

2G(h)
is equal to the curvature
F(h)
of
the connection

\mathbb{D}^{1}_{h}=\overline{\partial}_{E}+\theta_{h}^{\dagger}+\partial_{E,h}+\theta
.

2.6.2
The case
\boldsymbol{\lambda\neq 0}

Let
G(h)=G(h)^{2,0}+G(h)^{1,1}+G(h)^{0,2}

denote the decomposition into
(p,q)
-parts.
If
G(h)=0
,
we clearly obtain
G(h)^{1,1}=0
.
If
\lambda\neq 0
,
we obtain the converse.

Proposition 2.15
.

Suppose
\lambda\neq 0
. If
G(h)^{1,1}=0
,
we obtain
G(h)=0
, i.e.,

h
is a pluri-harmonic metric of
\big(E,\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\big)
.

Proof.

As in
[
49
, Lemma 2.28]
,
the following holds:

\displaystyle\overline{\lambda}^{-1}\partial_{E,h}^{2}+\lambda^{-1}\theta_{E,h}^{2}=0,\qquad\lambda^{-1}\overline{\partial}_{E,h}^{2}+\overline{\lambda}^{-1}\big(\theta_{E,h}^{\dagger}\big)^{2}=0.

(2.6)

It is easy to check that

\overline{\partial}_{E,h}^{2}=-(\partial_{E,h}^{2})^{\dagger}
,

\big(\theta_{E,h}^{\dagger}\big)^{2}=-(\theta_{E,h}^{2})^{\dagger}

and

\big(\overline{\partial}_{E,h}\theta_{E,h}\big)^{\dagger}=\partial_{E,h}\theta^{\dagger}_{E,h}
.

From the flatness
\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}\circ\mathbb{D}^{\lambda}=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle(\lambda\partial_{E,h}+\theta_{E,h})^{2}=\lambda^{2}\partial_{E,h}^{2}+\lambda\partial_{E,h}\theta_{E,h}+\theta_{E,h}^{2}=0,

(2.7)

\displaystyle\bigl[\overline{\partial}_{E,h}+\lambda\theta^{\dagger}_{E,h},\lambda\partial_{E,h}+\theta_{E,h}\bigr]
